```json
{
  "text": "Principles:\n1. Before each move, check opponent threats: hanging pieces, checks, captures.\n2. Develop knights and bishops early, castle by move 10, control the center.\n3. Never leave a piece undefended; check that the destination square is safe.\n6. Look for forks, pins and back-rank tricks, for both sides.\n7. In endgames, activate the king and push passed pawns.\n8. Resign only if clearly lost; otherwise keep setting problems.\n9. After each game, note what worked and what lost points."
}
```

> 3. Don't move the same piece twice in the opening without a reason.
> 4. Trade when ahead in material; avoid trades when behind.
> 5. Never leave a piece undefended; check that the destination square is safe.
> 6. Look for forks, pins and back-rank tricks, for both sides.
> 7. In endgames, activate the king and push passed pawns.
> 8. Resign only if clearly lost; otherwise keep setting problems.
> 9. After each game, note what worked and what lost points.

**Text**

I lost game 2. I was White and A01 won on material at the move cap. The match is now 1.5 to 0.5 in A01's favour, with games 3 and 4 still to play.

I lost the game through three tactical oversights:
- **Move 21 (Bc3):** I left my bishop undefended against the queen, and Black simply took it.
- **Move 29 (Qe3):** My queen had been guarding the rook on c2. I moved it away and the rook was left attacked, though I recaptured on the next move.
- **Move 38 (a4):** I moved a pawn while my queen sat on a5, where Black's queen could take it.

I did win the queen back at the end, but the last few moves were too late to make up the material.

I rewrote my strategy brain as version 3, within the 150-word limit. The new rules are:
- Check every move for my pieces that enemy lines attack.
- Ask what a defender was protecting before I move it.
- Prefer simple, solid moves.
